ASIDE 2.5 The polynomial FN .X; Y / was introduced by Kronecker more than 100
years ago. It is known to be symmetric in X and Y . For N D 2, it is
X3 C Y 3  X2Y 2 C 1488XY.X C Y /  162000.X2 C Y 2/
C 40773375XY C 8748000000.X C Y /  157464000000000:
It was computed for N D 3; 5; 7 by Smith (1878), Berwick (1916), and Herrmann (1974)
respectively. At this point the humans gave up, and left it to the computers, which found
F11 in 1984. This last computation took about 20 hours on a VAX-780, and the result is a
polynomial with coefﬁcients up to 1060 that takes ﬁve pages to write out. It is important
to know that the polynomial exists; fortunately, it is not important to know what it is.
The curve X0.N / over Q
Let CN be the afﬁne curve over Q with equation FN .X; Y / D 0, and let
NCN be the projective curve deﬁned by FN made homogeneous. Then z 7!
.j.z/; j.Nz// is a map X0.N / X  ! CN .C/, where  is the set where j
or jN has a pole. This map extends uniquely to a map X0.N / !
NCN .C/,
which is an isomorphism except over the singular points of NCN , and the pair
.X0.N /; X0.N / !
NCN .C// is uniquely determined by NCN (up to a unique
isomorphism): it is the canonical “desingularization” of NCN over C.
Now consider NCN over Q. There is a canonical desingularization X !
NCN over Q, i.e., a projective nonsingular curve X over Q, and a regular map
X ! NCN that is an isomorphism except over the singular points of NCN , and the
pair .X; X ! NCN / is uniquely determined by NCN (up to unique isomorphism).
When we pass to the C-points, we see that .X.C/; X.C/ ! NCN .C// has the
property characterizing .X0.N /; X0.N / ! NCN.C//, and so there is a unique
isomorphism of Riemann surfaces X0.N / ! X.C/ compatible with the maps
to NCN .C/.
In summary, we have a well-deﬁned curve X over Q, a regular map 	W X !
NCN over Q, and an isomorphism X0.N / ! X.C/ whose composite with 	.C/
is (outside a ﬁnite set) z 7! .j.z/; j.Nz//.
In future, we’ll often use X0.N / to denote the curve X over Q — it should
be clear from the context whether we mean the curve over Q or the Riemann
surface. The afﬁne curve X0.N / X fcuspsg  X0.N / is denoted Y0.N /; thus
Y0.N /.C/ D 0.1/nH.

singularities, formula (7), p. 34, would predict much too high a genus.

Modular forms
It is difﬁcult to construct functions on H invariant under a subgroup  of SL2.Z/
of ﬁnite index. One strategy is to construct functions, not invariant under  , but
transforming in a certain ﬁxed manner. Two functions transforming in the same
manner will be invariant under  . This idea suggests the notion of a modular
form.

Modular forms and the L-series of elliptic curves
In this section, I shall discuss how the L-series classify the elliptic curves over
Q up to isogeny, and then I shall explain how the work of Hecke, Petersson, and
Atkin-Lehner leads to a list of candidates for the L-series of such curves, and
hence suggests a classiﬁcation of the isogeny classes.
Dirichlet series
A Dirichlet series is a series of the form
f .s/ D
X
n1
a.n/ns;

The simplest example of such a series is, of course, the Riemann zeta function

the half-plane <.s/ > b.
It is important to note that the function f .s/ determines the a.n/s, i.e., if
P a.n/ns and P b.n/ns are equal as functions of s on some half-plane, then

a.n/ D b.n/ for all n. In fact, by means of the Mellin transform and its inverse

on some disk about 0, and g.q/ D P a.n/qn. Therefore, the claim follows from
the similar statement for power series.
We shall be especially interested in Dirichlet series that are equal to Euler

Dirichlet series arise in two essentially different ways: from analysis and
from arithmetic geometry and number theory. One of the big problems in math-
ematics is to show that the second set of Dirichlet series is a subset of the ﬁrst,
and to identify the subset. This is a major theme in the Langlands program,
and the rest of the book will be concerned with explaining how work of Wiles
and others succeeds in identifying the L-series of all elliptic curves over Q with
certain L-series attached to modular forms.

L-series and isogeny classes
Recall (p. 49) that two elliptic curves E and E0 are said to be isogenous if there
exists a nonconstant regular map from one to the other, and that isogeny is an
equivalence relation.
An isogeny E ! E0 deﬁnes a homomorphism E.Q/ ! E0.Q/ which, in
general, will be neither injective nor surjective, but which does have a ﬁnite
kernel and cokernel. Therefore, the ranks of E.Q/ and E0.Q/ are the same, but
their torsion subgroups will, in general, be different. Surprisingly, isogenous
curves over a ﬁnite ﬁeld do have the same number of points.
THEOREM 4.1 Let E and E0 be elliptic curves over Q. If E and E0 are isoge-
nous, then Np.E/ D Np.E0/ for all good p. Conversely, if Np.E/ D Np.E0/
for sufﬁciently many good p, then E is isogenous to E0.
PROOF. The fact that allows us to show that Np.E/ D Np.E0/ when E and
E0 are isogenous is that Np.E/ is the degree of a map E ! E, in fact, it is the
degree of '  1 where ' is the Frobenius map (see the proof of Theorem 9.4).
An isogeny ˛W E ! E0 induces an isogeny ˛pW Ep ! E0
p on the reductions of
the curves modulo p, which commutes with the Frobenius map: if
˛.x Wy Wz/ D .P.x; y; z/ W Q.x; y; z/ W R.x; y; z//;

so,

The converse is much more difﬁcult. It was conjectured by Tate about 1963,
and proved under some hypotheses by Serre. It was proved in general by Falt-
ings in his paper on Mordell’s conjecture (Faltings 1983).
2
Faltings’s result gives an effective procedure for deciding whether two el-
liptic curves over Q are isogenous: there is a constant P such that if Np.E/ D
Np.E0/ for all good p 	 P , then E and E0 are isogenous. This has been made
into an effective algorithm. In practice, if your computer fails to ﬁnd a p with
Np.E/ ¤ Np.E0/ in a few minutes you can be very conﬁdent that the curves
are isogenous.
It is not quite obvious, but it follows from the theory of N´eron models, that
isogenous elliptic curves have the same type of reduction at every prime. There-
fore, isogenous curves have exactly the same L-series and the same conductor.
Because the L-series is determined by, and determines the Np, we have the
following corollary.
COROLLARY 4.2 Two elliptic curves E and E0 are isogenous if and only if
L.E; s/ D L.E0; s/.
We therefore have a one-to-one correspondence:
fisogeny classes of elliptic curves over Qg $ f certain L-seriesg:
In the remainder of this section we shall identify the L-series arising from ellip-
tic curves over Q (in fact, we’ll even identify the L-series of the elliptic curves
with a ﬁxed conductor).
Since we shall be classifying elliptic curves only up to isogeny, it is worth
noting that a theorem of Shafarevich implies that there are only ﬁnitely many
isomorphism classes of elliptic curves over Q with a given conductor, hence
only ﬁnitely many in each isogeny class — see Silverman 1986, IX.6.

Linear algebra: the spectral theorem
Recall that a hermitian form on a vector space V is a mapping h ; iW V V ! C
such that hv; wi D hw; vi and h ; i is linear in one variable and conjugate-linear
in the other. Such a form is said to be positive-deﬁnite if hv; vi > 0 whenever
v ¤ 0. A linear map ˛W V ! V is self-adjoint (or hermitian) relative to h ; i if
h˛v; wi D hv; ˛wi;
all v; w:
THEOREM 4.16 (SPECTRAL THEOREM) Let V be a ﬁnite-dimensional com-
plex vector space with a positive-deﬁnite hermitian form h ; i.
(a) Any self-adjoint linear map ˛W V ! V is diagonalizable, i.e., V is a direct
sum of eigenspaces for ˛.
(b) Let ˛1; ˛2; : : : be a sequence of commuting self-adjoint linear maps V !
V ; then V has a basis of consisting of vectors that are eigenvectors for all
˛i.
PROOF. (a) Because C is algebraically closed, ˛ has an eigenvector e1. Let V1
be .Ce1/?. Then V1 is stable under ˛, and so contains an eigenvector e2. Let
V2 D .Ce1 ˚ Ce2/? etc..
(b) Now suppose V D L V.i/ where the i are the distinct eigenvalues of
˛1. Because ˛2 commutes with ˛1, it stabilizes each V.i/, and so each V.i/
can be decomposed into a direct sum of eigenspaces for ˛2. Continuing in this
fashion, we arrive at a decomposition V D L Vj such that each ˛i acts as a
scalar on each Vj . Choose bases for each Vj , and take their union.
2
This suggests that we should look for a hermitian form on S2k.0.N // for
which the T .n/’s are self-adjoint.

As Poincar´e pointed out,5 the unit disk forms a model for hyperbolic geometry:
if one deﬁnes a “line” to be a segment of a circle orthogonal to the circumference

New forms: the theorem of Atkin and Lehner
The problem left by the last subsection has a simple remedy. If M jN , then
0.M /  0.N /, and so S2k.0.M //  S2k.0.N //. Recall that the N turns
up in the functional equation for L.f; s/, and so it is not surprising that we run
into trouble when we mix f s of “level” N with f s that are really of level M jN ,
M < N .
The way out of the problem is to deﬁne a cusp form that is in some sub-
space S2k.0.M //, M jN , M < N , to be old. The old forms form a subspace
S old
2k .0.N // of S2k.0.N //, and the orthogonal complement S new
2k .0.N // is
called the space of new forms. It is stable under all the operators T .n/ and wN ,
and so S new
2k
decomposes into a direct sum of orthogonal subspaces Wi,
S new
2k .0.N // D L Wi
each of which is a simultaneous eigenspace for all T .n/ with gcd.n; N / D 1.
Since the T .p/ for pjN and wN each commute with the T .n/ for gcd.n; N / D
1, each stabilizes each Wi.
THEOREM 4.20 (ATKIN-LEHNER 1970) The spaces Wi in the above decom-
position all have dimension 1.
It follows that each Wi is also an eigenspace for wN and T .p/, pjN . Each
Wi contains (exactly) one cusp form f whose q-expansion is of the form q C
P
n2 c.n/qn.

.f; s/ satisﬁes a functional equation
.f; s/ D ".f; 2  s/
where " D ˙1 is the eigenvalue of wN acting on Wi. If the c.n/ 2 Z, then
.f; s/ is a candidate for being the L-function of an elliptic curve E over Q.

Statement of the main theorems
Recall that to an elliptic curve E over Q, we have attached an L-series
L.E; s/ D P anns that has coefﬁcients an 2 Z, can be expressed as an Euler
product, and (conjecturally) satisﬁes a functional equation (involving NE=Q, the
conductor on E). Moreover, isogenous elliptic curves have the same L-series.
We therefore have a map
E 7! L.E; s/W f elliptic curves=Qg=
 ! f Dirichlet seriesg:
An important theorem of Faltings (1983) shows that the map is injective: two
elliptic curves are isogenous if they have the same L-function.
On the other hand, the theory of Hecke and Petersson, together with the the-
orem of Atkin and Lehner, shows that the subspace S new
2
.0.N //  S2.0.N //
of new forms decomposes into a direct sum
S new
2
.0.N // D L Wi
of one-dimensional subspaces Wi that are simultaneous eigenspaces for all the
T .n/’s with gcd.n; N / D 1. Because they have dimension 1, each Wi is also
an eigenspace for wN and for the T .p/ with pjN . An element of one of the
subspaces Wi, i.e., a simultaneous eigenforms in S new
2
.0.N //, is traditionally
called a newform, and I’ll adopt this terminology.
In each Wi there is exactly one form fi D P c.n/qn with c.1/ D 1 (said to
be normalized). Because fi is an eigenform for all the Hecke operators, it has
an Euler product, and because it is an eigenform for wN , it satisﬁes a functional
equation. If the c.n/s are6 in Z, then L.fi; s/ is a candidate for being the L-
function of an elliptic curve over Q.
CONJECTURE 5.1 (MODULARITY, OR TANIYAMA, OR . . . ) A Dirichlet L-ser-
ies P c.n/ns, c.n/ 2 Z, is the L-series L.E; s/ of an elliptic curve over Q
with conductor N if and only if it is the L-series L.f; s/ of a normalized new-
form for 0.N /.
The next theorem proves the “if”.
THEOREM 5.2 (EICHLER-SHIMURA) Let f
D P c.n/qn be a normalized
newform for 0.N /. If all c.n/ 2 Z, then there exists an elliptic curve Ef
of conductor N such that L.Ef ; s/ D L.f; s/.

and that if they lie in Q then they lie in Z

5. STATEMENT OF THE MAIN THEOREMS
209
The early forms of the theorem were less precise — in particular, they
predate the work of Atkin and Lehner in which newforms were deﬁned.
The theorem of Eichler-Shimura has two parts: given f , construct the curve
Ef (up to isogeny); having constructed Ef , prove that L.Ef ; s/ D L.f; s/.
I’ll discuss the two parts in Sections 6 and 7.
After the theorem of Eichler-Shimura, to prove Conjecture 5.1, it remains
to show that every elliptic curve E arises from a modular form f — such an
elliptic curve is said to be modular.
In a set of problems circulated to those attending the famous 1955 Tokyo
and Nikko conference7, Taniyama asked (in somewhat vague form)8 whether
every elliptic curve was modular. In the ensuing years, this question was appar-
ently discussed by various people, including Shimura, who however published
nothing about it.
One can ask whether every Dirichlet L-series L.s/ D P anns, an 2 Z,
equal to an Euler product (of the same type as L.E; s/), and satisfying a func-
tional equation (of the same type as L.E; s/) must automatically be of the form
L.f; s/. Regarding this, Weil (1967) proved something only a little weaker. Let

map Z ! C by setting .m/ D .m mod n/ if m and n are relatively prime

Weil showed that if all the functions .s/ satisfy a functional equation relating
.s/ and .2k  s/ (and some other mild conditions), then L.s/ D L.f; s/
for some cusp form f of weight 2k for 0.N /. Weil also stated Conjecture 5.1
(as an exercise!) — this was its ﬁrst appearance in print.
Weil’s result showed that if L.E; s/ and its twists satisfy a functional equa-
tion of the correct form, then E is modular. Since the Hasse-Weil conjecture
was widely believed, Weil’s paper (for the ﬁrst time) gave a strong reason for
believing Conjecture 5.1, i.e., it made (5.1) into a conjecture rather than a ques-
tion.9 Also, for the ﬁrst time it related the level N of f to the conductor of E,
and so made it possible to test the conjecture numerically: list all the f ’s for
0.N /, list all isogeny classes of elliptic curves over Q with conductor N , and
see whether they match. A small industry grew up to do just that.
For several years, the conjecture was referred to as Weil’s conjecture. Then,
after Taniyama’s question was rediscovered, it was called the Taniyama-Weil
7They included Artin, Brauer, Chevalley, Deuring, Iwasawa, Nagata, N´eron, Satake, Serre,
Shimura, Taniyama, Weil . . . !
8“Let C be an elliptic curve deﬁned over an algebraic number ﬁeld k, and LC .s/ denote the

PDF PAGE 218
210
CHAPTER V. ELLIPTIC CURVES AND MODULAR FORMS
conjecture. Finally, after Lang adopted it as one of his pet projects10, it became
unsafe to call it anything other than the Shimura-Taniyama conjecture. More
recently, authors have referred to it as the modularity conjecture (now theorem).
In a lecture in 1985, Frey suggested that the curve in Exercise 4.21, de-
ﬁned by a counterexample to Fermat’s Last Theorem, should not be modular.
This encouraged Serre to rethink some old conjectures of his, and formulate
two conjectures, one of which implies that Frey’s curve is indeed not modular.
In 1986, Ribet proved sufﬁcient of Serre’s conjectures to be able to show that
Frey’s curve can’t be modular. I’ll discuss this work in Section 9.
Thus, at this stage (1986) it was known that Conjecture 5.1 for semistable
elliptic curves over Q implies Fermat’s Last Theorem, which inspired Wiles
to attempt to prove Conjecture 5.1. After a premature announcement in 1993,
Wiles proved in 1994 (with the help of R. Taylor) that all semistable elliptic
curves over Q are modular. Recall that semistable just means that the curve
doesn’t have cuspidal reduction at any prime. Breuil, Conrad, Diamond, and
Taylor improved the theorem so that it now says that all elliptic curves E over
Q is modular. In other words, the map
f 7! Ef W ff g ! fE over Qg=
is surjective. I’ll discuss the strategy of Wiles’s proof in Section 8.

Construction of the elliptic curve over Q
We brieﬂy explain why the above construction in fact gives an elliptic curve

For a compact Riemann surface X, we deﬁned

This is a complex manifold, but as in the case of an elliptic curve, it is possible
to construct enough functions on it to embed it into projective space, and so
realize it as a projective algebraic variety.
Now suppose X is a nonsingular projective curve over an ﬁeld k. Weil
showed (as part of the work mentioned on p. 159) that it is possible to attach to X
a projective algebraic variety Jac.X/ over k, which, in the case k D C becomes
the variety deﬁned in the last paragraph. There is again a map X ! Jac.X/,
well-deﬁned up to translation by the choice of a point P0 2 X.k/. The variety
Jac.X/ is an abelian variety, i.e., not only is it projective, but it also has a group
structure. (An abelian variety of dimension 1 is an elliptic curve.)
In particular, there is such a variety attached to the curve X0.N / deﬁned in
Section 2. Moreover (see the next section), the Hecke operators T .n/ deﬁne
endomorphisms of Jac.X0.N //. Because it has an abelian group structure, any
integer m deﬁnes an endomorphism of Jac.X0.N //, and we deﬁne Ef to be the
largest “quotient” of Jac.X0.N // on which T .n/ and c.n/ agree for all n rela-
tively prime to N . One can prove that this operation of “passing to the quotient”
commutes with change of the ground ﬁeld, and so in this way we obtain an el-
liptic curve over Q that becomes equal over C to the curve deﬁned in the last
subsection. On composing X0.N / ! Jac.X0.N // with Jac.X0.N // ! Ef
we obtain a map X0.N / ! Ef . In summary:

Brief review of the points of order p on elliptic curves
Let E be an elliptic curve over an algebraically closed ﬁeld k. The map pW E !
E (multiplication by p) is of degree p2. If k has characteristic zero, then the
map is separable, which implies that its kernel has order p2. If k has character-
istic p, the map is never separable: either it is purely inseparable (and so E has
no points of order p) or its separable and inseparable degrees are p (and so E
has p points of order dividing p). The ﬁrst case occurs for only ﬁnitely many
values of j.
The Eichler-Shimura relation
The curve X0.N / and the Hecke correspondence T .p/ are deﬁned over Q. For
almost all primes p −N , X0.N / will reduce to a nonsingular curve QX0.N /.12
For such a prime p, the correspondence T .p/ deﬁnes a correspondence QT .p/
on QX0.N /.
THEOREM 7.4 For a prime p where X0.N / has good reduction,

Wiles’s proof
Somebody with an average or even good

In this section, I explain the strategy of Wiles’s proof of the Taniyama con-
jecture for semistable elliptic curves over Q (i.e., curves with at worst nodal
reduction).
Recall, that if S denotes the sphere, then

Something similar is true for Q. Let K be a ﬁnite extension of Q, and let
OK be the ring of integers in K. In OK, the ideal pOK factors into a product of
powers of prime ideals: pOK D Q pep: The prime p is said to be unramiﬁed
in K if no ep > 1.
Now assume K=Q is Galois with Galois group G. Let p be prime, and
choose a prime ideal p dividing pOK (so that p \ Z D .p/). Let G.p/ be

CONJECTURE 8.5 (SERRE) Every odd irreducible representation  W GS !

“Odd” means that det .c/ D 1, where c is complex conjugation. “Irre-
ducible” means that there is no one-dimensional subspace of F2

the action of GS. Let E` D E.Qal/`. The Weil pairing (Silverman 1986, III.8),

shows that the representation of GS on E` is odd. It need not be irreducible; for
example, if E has a point of order ` with coordinates in Q, then it won’t be.
As we shall discuss in the next section, Serre in fact gave a recipe for deﬁn-
ing the level N and weight 2k of modular form.
By the early 1990s, there as much numerical evidence supporting Serre’s
conjecture, but few theorems.14 The most important of these was the following.
THEOREM 8.6 (LANGLANDS, TUNNELL) If  W GS ! GL2.F3/ is odd and
irreducible, then it is modular.
14There has been much progress on the conjecture since then.

Note that GL2.F3/ has order 8  6 D 48. The action of PGL2.F3/ on the
projective plane over F3 identiﬁes it with S4, and so GL2.F3/ is a double cover
QS4 of S4.
The theorem of Langlands and Tunnell in fact concerned representations
GS ! GL2.C/. In the nineteenth century, Klein classiﬁed the ﬁnite subgroups
of GL2.C/: their images in PGL2.C/ are cyclic, dihedral, A4, S4, or A5. Lang-
lands constructed candidates for the modular forms, and veriﬁed they had the
correct property in the A4 case. Tunnell veriﬁed this in the S4 case, and, since
GL2.F3/ embeds into GL2.C/, this veriﬁes Serre’s conjecture for F3.

It is now possible to explain Wiles’s strategy. First, state conditions  as

THEOREM 8.7 (WILES) The homomorphism ıW QR ! T is an isomorphism
(and so every -representation lifting 0 is modular).
Now let E be an elliptic curve over Q, and assume initially that the repre-
sentation of GS on E3 is irreducible. By the theorem of Langlands and Tunnell,
the representation 0W GS ! Aut.E.KS/3/ is modular, and so, by the theorem
of Wiles, 3W GS ! Aut.T3E/ is modular, which implies that E is modular.
What if the representation of GS on E.KS/3 is not irreducible, for example,
if E.Q/ contains a point of order three? It is not hard to show that the repre-
sentations of GS on E.KS/3 and E.KS/5 can’t both be reducible, because
otherwise either E or a curve isogenous to E will have rational points of order

226
CHAPTER V. ELLIPTIC CURVES AND MODULAR FORMS
3 and 5, hence a point of order 15, which is impossible. Unfortunately, there is
no Langlands-Tunnell theorem for 5. Instead, Wiles uses the following elegant
argument.
He shows that, given E, there exists a semistable elliptic curve E0 over Q
such that:
(a) E0.KS/3 is irreducible;
(b) E0.KS/5  E.KS/5 as GS-modules.
Because of (a), the preceding argument applies to E0 and shows it to be
modular. Hence the representation 5W GS ! Aut.T5E0/ is modular, which
implies that 0W GS ! Aut.E0.KS/5/  Aut.E.KS/5/ is modular. Now,
Wiles can apply his original argument with 3 replaced by 5.

Fermat, at last
Fix a prime number `, and let E be an elliptic curve over Q. For a prime p
it is possible to decide whether or not E has good reduction at p purely by
considering the action of G D Gal.Qal=Q/ on the modules E.Qal/`n, for all
n  1.
Let M be a ﬁnite abelian group, and let W G ! Aut.M / be a continuous
homomorphism (discrete topology on Aut.M /). The kernel H of  is an open
subgroup of G, and therefore its ﬁxed ﬁeld QalH is a ﬁnite extension of Q. We
say that  is unramiﬁed at p if p is unramiﬁed in QalH. With this terminology,
we can now state a converse to Proposition 8.1.
THEOREM 9.1 Let ` be a prime. The elliptic curve E has good reduction at p
if and only if the representation of G on E.Qal/`n is unramiﬁed for all n.
The proof makes use of the theory of N´eron models.
There is a similar criterion for p D `.
THEOREM 9.2 Let ` be a prime. The elliptic curve E has good reduction at `
if and only if the representation of G on E`n is ﬂat for all n.
For the experts, the representation of G on E.Qal/`n is ﬂat if there is a ﬁnite
ﬂat group scheme H over Z` such that H.Qal
` /  E.Qal
` /`n as G-modules.
Some authors say “ﬁnite” or “crystalline” instead of ﬂat.
These criteria show that it is possible to detect whether E has bad reduction
at p, and hence whether p divides the conductor of E, from knowing how G
acts on E.Qal/`n for all n — it may not be possible to detect bad reduction
simply by looking at E.Qal/` for example.
Recall that Serre conjectured that every odd irreducible representation W G !
GL2.F`/ is modular, i.e., that there exists an f D P c.n/qn 2 S2k.0.N //,

by Serre.
This proof is difﬁcult.

ASIDE 9.5 Great problems are important because, like the Riemann hypothesis, they
have important applications, or, like Fermat’s Last Theorem, they reveal our ignorance
and inspire great mathematics. Fermat’s Last Theorem has certainly inspired great math-
ematics but it needs to be said that, even after its solution, our ignorance of the rational
solutions of polynomial equations over Q remains almost as profound as before because
the method applies only to Fermat’s equation (or very similar equations). We don’t even
know, for example, whether it is possible for there to exist an algorithm for deciding
whether a polynomial equation with coefﬁcients in Q has a solution with coordinates in
Q.
NOTES Among the many works inspired by the proof of Fermat’s Last Theorem, I men-
tion only the book Mozzochi 2000, which gives an engaging eyewitness account of the
events surrounding the proof, the book Diamond and Shurman 2005, which gives a much
more detailed description of the modularity theorem and its background than that in this
chapter, and the book of the 1995 instructional conference (Cornell et al. 1997) devoted
to explaining the work of Ribet and others on Serre’s conjecture and of Wiles and others
on the modularity conjecture .
